What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well, it's... it's a lot of things, really, but the main reason I finally made the appointment—or, well, the reason my sister told me I had to make the appointment—is because of how I've been moving lately. It's not just how I feel on the inside, which is its own whole complicated mess, but it's what people can actually *see*. \n\nLike, at work, I'm on the phones all day, right? Customer service. And lately, it's like I'm moving through molasses. I'll be sitting in my chair, and just the thought of getting up to go to the breakroom feels like I'm trying to lift a hundred pounds. It takes me forever to just stand up. And my supervisor actually pulled me aside because he said there are these huge, awkward pauses before I answer customers, or even before I answer him when he asks me a simple question. It's like my brain and my body are just... underwater. \n\nBut then, it's not even always like that, which makes it even more confusing. Sometimes I swing completely the other way. I'll be at home in my apartment, and I can't sit down at all. I'll just pace back and forth from the kitchen to the living room, over and over, just wearing a path in the rug. And I do this thing with my hands—I just keep rubbing them together, wringing them, almost like I do when I've just had a really awful, screaming customer on the line and I'm trying to shake off the bad call. Except I'm not at work, and there's no call to shake off. It's just this restless, crawling feeling, and I can't stop moving. So, it's either I'm a statue or I'm a wind-up toy, and people are starting to stare, and... yeah, that's why I'm here.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling depressed most of the day nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean... yeah. It's pretty much all day, every day. It's like this heavy, gray blanket just sort of draped over everything. And it's been like that for... well, most of the time since all of this really started. I mean, sure, there are some weeks where it feels a little lighter, where maybe the blanket is just a sheet, you know? But it's always there in the background. \n\nIt's not just the sadness, either. It's this awful guilt that just eats at me, like I'm doing everything wrong, even when I'm just sitting there. And I used to love playing video games, or even just going for a walk, but now... I just don't care. I have zero interest in anything. And the sleep—don't even get me started on the sleep. I'll toss and turn, or I'll wake up at 3 AM and just stare at the ceiling, thinking about every mistake I've ever made. So yeah, even on the days where I can force myself to smile for a customer on the phone, all of that—the sadness, the guilt, the not caring, the terrible sleep—it's just always humming right there in the background, almost every single day.\n\nDoctor: Have you been hearing voices that other people can’t hear?\n\nPatient: Well, see, that's... that's another thing that's been really freaking me out, honestly. Because I know I'm at home, right? I live alone, so it's usually pretty quiet. But, you know how I mentioned I work on the phones all day? We use this specific routing software, and when a call comes in, there's this very distinct, sharp little double-beep in the headset. It's designed to jolt your attention so you don't miss the metric for answering fast enough. People who work in call centers, they always joke about hearing the beep in their sleep, which I guess is normal. \n\nBut lately... it's not just like a memory of the sound. It's happening when I'm completely logged off. Like, my work laptop will be completely shut down, closed flat on the coffee table, and my cell phone is on silent, so there is absolutely no phone ringing anywhere in my apartment. And I'll hear it. I'll hear that exact old headset beep, clear as day, right in my ear. \n\nAnd then... yeah, it's not just the beep. There's a voice, too. It sounds exactly like a supervisor—not necessarily my current supervisor, just this very authoritative, kind of impatient, middle-management type voice. And it'll just say my name. Just out of nowhere. Or sometimes—and this is the one that really gets my heart racing and makes my stomach drop—it'll just say, \"too slow.\" \n\nI'll physically spin around, thinking someone is in the room with me, or maybe I somehow left my computer on and a manager is talking through the speakers, which doesn't even make technical sense because our system doesn't have open mics like that. But I'll check anyway. I'll go over, open the laptop, check the screen, check my phone. Nothing. It's just me in the living room. But it's so incredibly vivid. It's not like a thought inside my head, you know? It sounds like someone is standing right behind my left shoulder, judging me. I'll just be trying to make a sandwich or something, laptop closed, completely off the clock, and I'll hear \"too slow.\" It's incredibly distracting, and it really makes it hard to just relax in my own space when I keep thinking someone is right there monitoring me.\n\nDoctor: Have the voice and the beep ever happened during a time when you were not feeling depressed?\n\nPatient: Actually, yeah. That’s... that’s the part that really messes with my head, to be completely honest with you. Because you’d think, right? You’d think that if I’m feeling better, if that heavy gray blanket I was talking about earlier finally lifts a little bit, that the rest of the weird stuff would just pack up and go away too. But it doesn’t. \n\nThere have been these stretches... like, I had a good two or three weeks a little while back where my mood was actually closer to normal. I wasn't waking up at 3 AM hating myself, and that awful guilt was just gone. I was actually getting things done around the apartment. I finally folded this massive pile of laundry that had been sitting on my bedroom chair for a month, I went to the actual grocery store instead of just ordering delivery and feeling terrible about the fees... I just felt okay. Productive, even. I was actually enjoying my evenings again.\n\nBut even then, during those few weeks when I was actually functioning and not feeling depressed at all, I still heard it. I’d be putting away my shirts, feeling pretty decent about my day, and then I'd hear the beep. And that same supervisor voice would just chime in right over my shoulder, clear as day. Or I’d just get this overwhelming, undeniable feeling that the old ticketing software we used to use at my last job was actively flagging me for being away from my desk, even though I was literally just in my own kitchen making dinner, completely off the clock, and feeling totally fine. \n\nIt’s so frustrating because I’ll be having a good week, a really productive stretch where the sadness is just gone, and I’ll think, okay, I’m back on track, I’m doing great. And then out of nowhere, that voice is there telling me I’m too slow, or I'm suddenly convinced I'm getting flagged by a software system that isn't even installed on my current laptop. So yeah, it definitely happens even when the depression isn't dragging\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period where you felt abnormally high, euphoric, or unusually energetic for several days in a row?\n\nPatient: No, no, I've never had anything like that. I mean, honestly, the idea of feeling euphoric or having too much energy is almost laughable to me right now. Like I said, even when I'm having a good week, it's just... okay. It's just normal. It's just me being able to fold my laundry or go to the grocery store without feeling like I'm dragging a hundred-pound weight behind me. It's never this big, grand, on-top-of-the-world feeling. \n\nI've seen people like that, though, you know? Back when I was in college—before I dropped out, obviously—I had this roommate, Greg. Greg was a business major, and I swear the guy never slept. He would go on these benders where he wasn't even drinking, he was just... wired. He’d start three new clubs, rearrange all the furniture in our dorm at 4 AM, and talk so fast you could barely understand him. It was exhausting just being around him. But me? No, I've never been like Greg. I've never had that kind of endless battery or felt like I could conquer the world. \n\nFor me, a high is just... not feeling terrible. It's just waking up and thinking, \"Okay, I can get through today,\" instead of, \"I wish I hadn't woken up at all.\" So, no, definitely no periods of being unusually energetic or euphoric. Just the heavy gray blanket, or the times when the blanket is gone but the weird headset beeps and the supervisor voice are still there. Never any kind of super-energy.\n\nDoctor: Have the voice and the feeling that you’re being monitored ever continued for at least two weeks during a time when you were not depressed?\n\nPatient: Yeah, like I was saying just a few minutes ago, it absolutely has. That specific stretch I mentioned, where I finally tackled that massive mountain of laundry and actually went down to the physical grocery store instead of using the app? That good period lasted for, I'd say, a solid two and a half, maybe three weeks. I know for a fact it was at least two weeks because I remember I had two full pay periods at work where I didn't call out sick a single time, which was a really huge deal for me. \n\nI was getting my chores done, I was cooking actual meals on the stove instead of just microwaving frozen things, and my mood was honestly the closest to normal it's been in a really long time. I wasn't waking up with that crushing dread or that heavy blanket feeling at all. I was just... functioning. \n\nBut even with all that going so well, for that entire two or three week stretch, the other stuff just didn't stop at all. I was still hearing that supervisor voice tell me I was \"too slow\" while I was literally just standing at the sink washing a frying pan. And that intense, nagging feeling I told you about, where I was completely sure the old ticketing software from my previous job was somehow running in the background and flagging me for being away from my desk? That stayed with me the whole time, too. Every single day of those few weeks, even though I wasn't depressed in the slightest, I was still dealing with the voice and that monitoring feeling. \n\nIt’s honestly so baffling to me, because you’d think they’d be connected, right? Like, common sense says if the sadness goes away, the voice should go away with it. You'd think it's all part of the same package. But they just... don't. It's like my mood completely fixed itself for those couple of weeks, but my brain was still stuck on the clock, just constantly checking over my shoulder. So, yeah, it definitely continued for well over two weeks while I was feeling totally fine.\n\nDoctor: Before this current period, have you ever had another episode in your life where you felt depressed like this for at least two weeks and then it went away?\n\nPatient: Honestly, no, I... I don't think so. I mean, I've really been racking my brain trying to figure out when all of this actually started, and looking back, I can't pinpoint any time before this current stretch where I felt anywhere near this bad for that long. Like, sure, everyone has bad days, right? You have a bad weekend, or maybe you get a flat tire, or fail a test—like back when I was still taking classes—and you feel like garbage for a few days. You stay in bed, you eat too much takeout, you ignore your texts for a bit. I’ve definitely had that happen. Especially when I was working at my last job, the one with that awful ticketing system I keep bringing up. There were definitely days where I was just completely drained and miserable after dealing with angry customers for eight hours straight. \n\nBut it was never... it never stretched into two whole weeks of this heavy, suffocating gray blanket, you know? It was always just a passing thing. I'd feel down for a couple of days, and then I'd catch up on sleep, or I'd have a good shift where people were actually nice to me on the phone, and I'd just bounce back. I'd be right back to my normal self. I've never had a time in my life, before this whole current situation started, where I was just swallowed up by that crushing guilt and sadness for weeks on end, and then had it just magically lift and go away. \n\nThis is really the first time I've ever been stuck in a hole this deep. It’s why it’s been so hard to deal with, honestly, because I don't have any kind of reference point for it. I keep waiting for it to just be a bad weekend that I can sleep off, but it's not. It just keeps dragging. So, yeah, to answer your question, no, I haven't had anything like this happen in the past. It's all just this one, big, long current thing.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any seizures?\n\nPatient: No, no seizures. I mean, I've never had anything like that happen to me. I know what they look like, though, because my aunt—my dad's sister, Aunt Linda—she has epilepsy, and I remember being at a family barbecue when I was maybe, I don't know, ten or eleven? And she had a seizure right there on the patio. It was terrifying, honestly, just watching her shake and not being able to do anything. The paramedics had to come and everything. It really stuck with me, you know? Just how sudden it was. But for me, personally? No, nothing like that. My body doesn't do that. I mean, I get the heavy feeling, like I said, where it's hard to move, but I'm fully conscious and I'm not shaking or losing control of my muscles or anything. It's just the tiredness. And the voices and the monitoring thing, of course, but physically, my brain isn't short-circuiting like that. I've never passed out or had convulsions or anything even close to a seizure. So, no, definitely not. Just... just the other stuff.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a serious head injury?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't. I mean, I've bumped my head a few times over the years, like anyone else, right? I remember one time, gosh, I must have been maybe twelve or thirteen, and I was riding my bike down this huge hill near my childhood home—it was this really steep road, completely paved but just a massive drop—and I hit this patch of gravel near the bottom. I went flying right over the handlebars. Scraped up my knees something awful, like, completely ruined my favorite pair of jeans, and I definitely hit my head on the pavement. I was wearing a helmet, thank goodness, because my mom was always so strict about that. She wouldn't even let me out of the driveway without it. I remember the helmet actually cracked a little bit on the side, which just goes to show how hard I hit. \n\nBut even then, I didn't black out or anything. I was just really dazed and my ears were ringing for a few minutes. My mom took me to the urgent care just to be safe, and they shined the little light in my eyes and made me follow their finger, and they said I was totally fine. No concussion, no nothing. Just a headache that lasted for the rest of the evening. I just laid on the couch with an ice pack and watched TV. \n\nAnd other than that, I really can't think of anything. I've never been in a car accident where I hit the windshield or the steering wheel. I've never played any contact sports—I was always more of a band kid, honestly, played the trumpet for a few years before I realized I didn't actually like it. So I never played football or hockey where you're constantly getting tackled or checked into the boards. I've never fallen off a ladder or had anything heavy drop on me at work. \n\nSo, no, nothing serious at all. Just normal clumsy stuff, like standing up too fast and hitting my head on an open cabinet door in the kitchen. I did that just last week, actually, while I was trying to put away some coffee mugs. Hurt like crazy for a second, but definitely not a serious injury. My brain is physically fine, as far as I know. Never had any kind of trauma or anything that would cause damage up there. Just the regular bumps and bruises of life, you know?\n\nDoctor: Have there been times when other people said your behavior or speech seemed very disorganized or bizarre?\n\nPatient: Well, I... I don't think anyone has ever come right out and used the word \"bizarre,\" but I know I've had moments where things get a little weird. Like, when I get really overwhelmed—which happens more often lately with everything going on—I kind of just... short-circuit a little bit? I don't know how else to describe it. \n\nLike, the other day, I was making coffee, and I poured it, and I was standing there in the kitchen, and I just sort of froze with the mug halfway to my mouth. I don't even know how long I was standing there like that. My roommate, Greg—he works from home, so he's always around—he came into the kitchen to grab a snack, and he was like, \"Uh, dude, you good?\" And it totally snapped me out of it, but I realized my arm was actually starting to cramp because I'd been holding the mug in mid-air for so long. I just told him I was lost in thought, but it felt a lot heavier than that, you know? \n\nAnd then there are times when I catch myself just pacing. Not even pacing like I'm trying to figure something out, but just walking this same exact little loop in the living room, right by the edge of the couch and the coffee table. Over and over. I won't even realize I'm doing it until I notice the pattern on the rug looks blurry because I've been staring at the same spot on the floor for ten minutes straight. Greg has definitely noticed that, too. He'll be on his laptop on the couch, and he'll finally look up and say, \"Man, you're wearing a hole in the floor, what are you doing?\" And I never really have a good answer for him. I just say I'm restless, but it feels more like I'm just stuck on a loop and I can't figure out how to stop walking. \n\nSo, I don't know if that counts as disorganized or bizarre, but it's definitely not how I normally act. It's just... when the pressure builds up and the thoughts get too loud, my body just sort of does these weird, repetitive things without me really deciding to do them. It's kind of embarrassing, honestly, especially when someone else points it out, because then I realize how strange I must look. But it's not like I'm running around screaming or talking in gibberish or anything. It's more just... getting stuck. Like a record skipping.\n\nDoctor: For at least one month, have you been hearing the voice or feeling monitored on most days?\n\nPatient: Yeah, oh yeah, definitely for at least a month. It's been way longer than that, honestly. I've been trying to keep track, just in my head, you know, because it's hard to write things down when you feel like someone might find the notebook or something. But if I really sit down and trace it back, the voices and that whole feeling of being monitored, being tracked... it started, gosh, about a year and eight months ago. \n\nI remember because it was right around the time my lease was up at my old apartment, the one over on 4th Street, and I was dealing with all the stress of deciding whether to renew or find a new place with Greg. And at first, I thought maybe it was just the stress of moving, you know? Like, your brain plays tricks on you when you're packing boxes until 2 AM and living off takeout. But then we moved, and we got settled into the new place, and the feeling didn't go away. If anything, it got worse. The voices started getting more frequent, just this constant murmur in the background, commenting on what I was doing or telling me I was being watched. \n\nAnd the thing is, it's not just when I'm feeling really, really down. Like, I know I get those periods where everything is just heavy and dark and I can barely get out of bed—those depressive slumps, right? But the voices and the tracking stuff, they don't just happen then. They're there even when my mood is... well, relatively okay. Like, I could be having an alright day, maybe even managing to get some work done on the phones for customer service, talking to people about their billing issues, and I'll still hear it. I'll still feel that prickle on the back of my neck like the camera on my laptop is on, even when I've put a piece of tape over it. \n\nSo it's not just tied to the sadness. It's its own separate thing that just... runs parallel to it, I guess? Or sometimes they overlap and it's a total mess, but yeah, it's been a year and eight months of this, pretty much most days. It's exhausting, honestly, just carrying it around all the time, trying to pretend everything is normal when you're constantly looking over your shoulder or trying to tune out someone talking to you when there's nobody in the room. But yeah, definitely way more than a month.\n\nDoctor: Have people told you that your face or voice seems unusually flat or emotionless?\n\nPatient: Yeah, actually, they have. It's funny you ask that, because just a couple of weeks ago, my buddy Mark—we took some college classes together back in the day—he brought it up. He was telling me this whole long story about his dog needing surgery. And I mean, I love that dog, he's this super sweet golden retriever mix, so I really did care about what he was saying. But apparently, I wasn't reacting the way I was supposed to. Mark literally stopped in the middle of his story and was like, \"Dude, are you even listening? You're acting like a robot.\" He told me that my voice just sounds completely flat now, and that my face is totally hard to read. Like I'm just staring right through him. \n\nAnd the thing is, I was listening! I felt awful for the dog. But I guess the emotion just doesn't translate to the outside anymore. I just find myself sitting there, and even when I'm genuinely invested in the conversation, I mostly just give these really short answers. Just, like, \"Yeah,\" or \"Wow,\" or \"Okay.\" I just don't have the energy to put into the facial expressions or the tone of voice, you know? It's like the bridge between what I'm feeling inside and what my face is doing is just out of order. \n\nAnd honestly, because of that whole dynamic, it just makes hanging out with people feel like this massive, exhausting chore. So lately, I just avoid making plans altogether. Greg will ask if I want to go grab a drink down the street, or Mark will want to come over to watch a game, and I just make up some excuse to stay home. It's just so much easier to stay in my room than to have to constantly try and force myself to look animated so my friends don't think I'm ignoring them. I just... I don't have the battery for it anymore.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Just a headache that lasted for the rest of the evening. I just laid on the couch with an ice pack and watched TV. \n\nAnd other than that, I really can't think of anything. I've never been in a car accident where I hit the windshield or the steering wheel. I've never played any contact sports—I was always more of a band kid, honestly, played the trumpet for a few years before I realized I didn't actually like it. So I never played football or hockey where you're constantly getting tackled or checked into the boards. I've never fallen off a ladder or had anything heavy drop on me at work. \n\nSo, no, nothing serious at all. Just normal clumsy stuff, like standing up too fast and hitting my head on an open cabinet door in the kitchen. I did that just last week, actually, while I was trying to put away some coffee mugs. Hurt like crazy for a second, but definitely not a serious injury. My brain is physically fine, as far as I know. Never had any kind of trauma or anything that would cause damage up there. Just the regular bumps and bruises of life, you know?\n\nDoctor: Have there been times when other people said your behavior or speech seemed very disorganized or bizarre?\n\nPatient: Well, I... I don't think anyone has ever come right out and used the word \"bizarre,\" but I know I've had moments where things get a little weird. Like, when I get really overwhelmed—which happens more often lately with everything going on—I kind of just... short-circuit a little bit? I don't know how else to describe it. \n\nLike, the other day, I was making coffee, and I poured it, and I was standing there in the kitchen, and I just sort of froze with the mug halfway to my mouth. I don't even know how long I was standing there like that. My roommate, Greg—he works from home, so he's always around—he came into the kitchen to grab a snack, and he was like, \"Uh, dude, you good?\" And it totally snapped me out of it, but I realized my arm was actually starting to cramp because I'd been holding the mug in mid-air for so long. I just told him I was lost in thought, but it felt a lot heavier than that, you know? \n\nAnd then there are times when I catch myself just pacing. Not even pacing like I'm trying to figure something out, but just walking this same exact little loop in the living room, right by the edge of the couch and the coffee table. Over and over. I won't even realize I'm doing it until I notice the pattern on the rug looks blurry because I've been staring at the same spot on the floor for ten minutes straight. Greg has definitely noticed that, too. He'll be on his laptop on the couch, and he'll finally look up and say, \"Man, you're wearing a hole in the floor, what are you doing?\" And I never really have a good answer for him. I just say I'm restless, but it feels more like I'm just stuck on a loop and I can't figure out how to stop walking. \n\nSo, I don't know if that counts as disorganized or bizarre, but it's definitely not how I normally act. It's just... when the pressure builds up and the thoughts get too loud, my body just sort of does these weird, repetitive things without me really deciding to do them. It's kind of embarrassing, honestly, especially when someone else points it out, because then I realize how strange I must look. But it's not like I'm running around screaming or talking in gibberish or anything. It's more just... getting stuck. Like a record skipping.\n\nDoctor: For at least one month, have you been hearing the voice or feeling monitored on most days?\n\nPatient: Yeah, oh yeah, definitely for at least a month. It's been way longer than that, honestly. I've been trying to keep track, just in my head, you know, because it's hard to write things down when you feel like someone might find the notebook or something. But if I really sit down and trace it back, the voices and that whole feeling of being monitored, being tracked... it started, gosh, about a year and eight months ago. \n\nI remember because it was right around the time my lease was up at my old apartment, the one over on 4th Street, and I was dealing with all the stress of deciding whether to renew or find a new place with Greg. And at first, I thought maybe it was just the stress of moving, you know? Like, your brain plays tricks on you when you're packing boxes until 2 AM and living off takeout. But then we moved, and we got settled into the new place, and the feeling didn't go away. If anything, it got worse. The voices started getting more frequent, just this constant murmur in the background, commenting on what I was doing or telling me I was being watched. \n\nAnd the thing is, it's not just when I'm feeling really, really down. Like, I know I get those periods where everything is just heavy and dark and I can barely get out of bed—those depressive slumps, right? But the voices and the tracking stuff, they don't just happen then. They're there even when my mood is... well, relatively okay. Like, I could be having an alright day, maybe even managing to get some work done on the phones for customer service, talking to people about their billing issues, and I'll still hear it. I'll still feel that prickle on the back of my neck like the camera on my laptop is on, even when I've put a piece of tape over it. \n\nSo it's not just tied to the sadness. It's its own separate thing that just... runs parallel to it, I guess? Or sometimes they overlap and it's a total mess, but yeah, it's been a year and eight months of this, pretty much most days. It's exhausting, honestly, just carrying it around all the time, trying to pretend everything is normal when you're constantly looking over your shoulder or trying to tune out someone talking to you when there's nobody in the room. But yeah, definitely way more than a month.\n\nDoctor: Have people told you that your face or voice seems unusually flat or emotionless?\n\nPatient: Yeah, actually, they have. It's funny you ask that, because just a couple of weeks ago, my buddy Mark—we took some college classes together back in the day—he brought it up. He was telling me this whole long story about his dog needing surgery. And I mean, I love that dog, he's this super sweet golden retriever mix, so I really did care about what he was saying. But apparently, I wasn't reacting the way I was supposed to. Mark literally stopped in the middle of his story and was like, \"Dude, are you even listening? You're acting like a robot.\" He told me that my voice just sounds completely flat now, and that my face is totally hard to read. Like I'm just staring right through him. \n\nAnd the thing is, I was listening! I felt awful for the dog. But I guess the emotion just doesn't translate to the outside anymore. I just find myself sitting there, and even when I'm genuinely invested in the conversation, I mostly just give these really short answers. Just, like, \"Yeah,\" or \"Wow,\" or \"Okay.\" I just don't have the energy to put into the facial expressions or the tone of voice, you know? It's like the bridge between what I'm feeling inside and what my face is doing is just out of order. \n\nAnd honestly, because of that whole dynamic, it just makes hanging out with people feel like this massive, exhausting chore. So lately, I just avoid making plans altogether. Greg will ask if I want to go grab a drink down the street, or Mark will want to come over to watch a game, and I just make up some excuse to stay home. It's just so much easier to stay in my room than to have to constantly try and force myself to look animated so my friends don't think I'm ignoring them. I just... I don't have the battery for it anymore.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

  },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, if I really sit down and think about the whole timeline... you know, going back to that whole apartment move a year and eight months ago when the voices and the monitoring stuff really kicked off... yeah. And I do this thing with my hands—I just keep rubbing them together, wringing them, almost like I do when I've just had a really awful, screaming customer on the line and I'm trying to shake off the bad call. Except I'm not at work, and there's no call to shake off. It's just this restless, crawling feeling, and I can't stop moving. So, it's either I'm a statue or I'm a wind-up toy, and people are starting to stare, and... yeah, that's why I'm here.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling depressed most of the day nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean... yeah. It's pretty much all day, every day. It's like this heavy, gray blanket just sort of draped over everything. And it's been like that for... well, most of the time since all of this really started. I mean, sure, there are some weeks where it feels a little lighter, where maybe the blanket is just a sheet, you know? But it's always there in the background. \n\nIt's not just the sadness, either. It's this awful guilt that just eats at me, like I'm doing everything wrong, even when I'm just sitting there. And I used to love playing video games, or even just going for a walk, but now... I just don't care. I have zero interest in anything. And the sleep—don't even get me started on the sleep. I'll toss and turn, or I'll wake up at 3 AM and just stare at the ceiling, thinking about every mistake I've ever made. So yeah, even on the days where I can force myself to smile for a customer on the phone, all of that—the sadness, the guilt, the not caring, the terrible sleep—it's just always humming right there in the background, almost every single day.\n\nDoctor: Have you been hearing voices that other people can’t hear?\n\nPatient: Well, see, that's... that's another thing that's been really freaking me out, honestly. Because I know I'm at home, right? I live alone, so it's usually pretty quiet. But, you know how I mentioned I work on the phones all day? We use this specific routing software, and when a call comes in, there's this very distinct, sharp little double-beep in the headset. It's designed to jolt your attention so you don't miss the metric for answering fast enough. People who work in call centers, they always joke about hearing the beep in their sleep, which I guess is normal. \n\nBut lately... it's not just like a memory of the sound. It's happening when I'm completely logged off. Like, my work laptop will be completely shut down, closed flat on the coffee table, and my cell phone is on silent, so there is absolutely no phone ringing anywhere in my apartment. And I'll hear it. I'll hear that exact old headset beep, clear as day, right in my ear. \n\nAnd then... yeah, it's not just the beep. There's a voice, too. It sounds exactly like a supervisor—not necessarily my current supervisor, just this very authoritative, kind of impatient, middle-management type voice. And it'll just say my name. Just out of nowhere. Or sometimes—and this is the one that really gets my heart racing and makes my stomach drop—it'll just say, \"too slow.\" \n\nI'll physically spin around, thinking someone is in the room with me, or maybe I somehow left my computer on and a manager is talking through the speakers, which doesn't even make technical sense because our system doesn't have open mics like that. But I'll check anyway. I'll go over, open the laptop, check the screen, check my phone. Nothing. It's just me in the living room. But it's so incredibly vivid. It's not like a thought inside my head, you know? It sounds like someone is standing right behind my left shoulder, judging me. I'll just be trying to make a sandwich or something, laptop closed, completely off the clock, and I'll hear \"too slow.\" It's incredibly distracting, and it really makes it hard to just relax in my own space when I keep thinking someone is right there monitoring me.\n\nDoctor: Have the voice and the beep ever happened during a time when you were not feeling depressed?\n\nPatient: Actually, yeah. That’s... that’s the part that really messes with my head, to be completely honest with you. Because you’d think, right? You’d think that if I’m feeling better, if that heavy gray blanket I was talking about earlier finally lifts a little bit, that the rest of the weird stuff would just pack up and go away too. But it doesn’t. \n\nThere have been these stretches... like, I had a good two or three weeks a little while back where my mood was actually closer to normal. I wasn't waking up at 3 AM hating myself, and that awful guilt was just gone. I was actually getting things done around the apartment. I finally folded this massive pile of laundry that had been sitting on my bedroom chair for a month, I went to the actual grocery store instead of just ordering delivery and feeling terrible about the fees... I just felt okay. Productive, even. I was actually enjoying my evenings again.\n\nBut even then, during those few weeks when I was actually functioning and not feeling depressed at all, I still heard it. I’d be putting away my shirts, feeling pretty decent about my day, and then I'd hear the beep. And that same supervisor voice would just chime in right over my shoulder, clear as day. Or I’d just get this overwhelming, undeniable feeling that the old ticketing software we used to use at my last job was actively flagging me for being away from my desk, even though I was literally just in my own kitchen making dinner, completely off the clock, and feeling totally fine. \n\nIt’s so frustrating because I’ll be having a good week, a really productive stretch where the sadness is just gone, and I’ll think, okay, I’m back on track, I’m doing great. And then out of nowhere, that voice is there telling me I’m too slow, or I'm suddenly convinced I'm getting flagged by a software system that isn't even installed on my current laptop. So yeah, it definitely happens even when the depression isn't dragging\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period where you felt abnormally high, euphoric, or unusually energetic for several days in a row?\n\nPatient: No, no, I've never had anything like that. I mean, honestly, the idea of feeling euphoric or having too much energy is almost laughable to me right now. Like I said, even when I'm having a good week, it's just... okay. It's just normal. It's just me being able to fold my laundry or go to the grocery store without feeling like I'm dragging a hundred-pound weight behind me. It's never this big, grand, on-top-of-the-world feeling. \n\nI've seen people like that, though, you know? Back when I was in college—before I dropped out, obviously—I had this roommate, Greg. Greg was a business major, and I swear the guy never slept. He would go on these benders where he wasn't even drinking, he was just... wired. He’d start three new clubs, rearrange all the furniture in our dorm at 4 AM, and talk so fast you could barely understand him. It was exhausting just being around him. But me? No, I've never been like Greg. I've never had that kind of endless battery or felt like I could conquer the world. \n\nFor me, a high is just... not feeling terrible. It's just waking up and thinking, \"Okay, I can get through today,\" instead of, \"I wish I hadn't woken up at all.\" So, no, definitely no periods of being unusually energetic or euphoric. Just the heavy gray blanket, or the times when the blanket is gone but the weird headset beeps and the supervisor voice are still there. Never any kind of super-energy.\n\nDoctor: Have the voice and the feeling that you’re being monitored ever continued for at least two weeks during a time when you were not depressed?\n\nPatient: Yeah, like I was saying just a few minutes ago, it absolutely has. That specific stretch I mentioned, where I finally tackled that massive mountain of laundry and actually went down to the physical grocery store instead of using the app? That good period lasted for, I'd say, a solid two and a half, maybe three weeks. I know for a fact it was at least two weeks because I remember I had two full pay periods at work where I didn't call out sick a single time, which was a really huge deal for me. \n\nI was getting my chores done, I was cooking actual meals on the stove instead of just microwaving frozen things, and my mood was honestly the closest to normal it's been in a really long time. I wasn't waking up with that crushing dread or that heavy blanket feeling at all. I was just... functioning. \n\nBut even with all that going so well, for that entire two or three week stretch, the other stuff just didn't stop at all. I was still hearing that supervisor voice tell me I was \"too slow\" while I was literally just standing at the sink washing a frying pan. And that intense, nagging feeling I told you about, where I was completely sure the old ticketing software from my previous job was somehow running in the background and flagging me for being away from my desk? That stayed with me the whole time, too. Every single day of those few weeks, even though I wasn't depressed in the slightest, I was still dealing with the voice and that monitoring feeling. \n\nIt’s honestly so baffling to me, because you’d think they’d be connected, right? Like, common sense says if the sadness goes away, the voice should go away with it. You'd think it's all part of the same package. But they just... don't. It's like my mood completely fixed itself for those couple of weeks, but my brain was still stuck on the clock, just constantly checking over my shoulder. So, yeah, it definitely continued for well over two weeks while I was feeling totally fine.\n\nDoctor: Before this current period, have you ever had another episode in your life where you felt depressed like this for at least two weeks and then it went away?\n\nPatient: Honestly, no, I... I don't think so. I mean, I've really been racking my brain trying to figure out when all of this actually started, and looking back, I can't pinpoint any time before this current stretch where I felt anywhere near this bad for that long. Like, sure, everyone has bad days, right? You have a bad weekend, or maybe you get a flat tire, or fail a test—like back when I was still taking classes—and you feel like garbage for a few days. You stay in bed, you eat too much takeout, you ignore your texts for a bit. I’ve definitely had that happen. Especially when I was working at my last job, the one with that awful ticketing system I keep bringing up. There were definitely days where I was just completely drained and miserable after dealing with angry customers for eight hours straight. \n\nBut it was never... it never stretched into two whole weeks of this heavy, suffocating gray blanket, you know? It was always just a passing thing. I'd feel down for a couple of days, and then I'd catch up on sleep, or I'd have a good shift where people were actually nice to me on the phone, and I'd just bounce back. I'd be right back to my normal self. I've never had a time in my life, before this whole current situation started, where I was just swallowed up by that crushing guilt and sadness for weeks on end, and then had it just magically lift and go away. \n\nThis is really the first time I've ever been stuck in a hole this deep. It’s why it’s been so hard to deal with, honestly, because I don't have any kind of reference point for it. I keep waiting for it to just be a bad weekend that I can sleep off, but it's not. It just keeps dragging. So, yeah, to answer your question, no, I haven't had anything like this happen in the past. It's all just this one, big, long current thing.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any seizures?\n\nPatient: No, no seizures. I mean, I've never had anything like that happen to me. I know what they look like, though, because my aunt—my dad's sister, Aunt Linda—she has epilepsy, and I remember being at a family barbecue when I was maybe, I don't know, ten or eleven? And she had a seizure right there on the patio. It was terrifying, honestly, just watching her shake and not being able to do anything. The paramedics had to come and everything. It really stuck with me, you know? Just how sudden it was. But for me, personally? No, nothing like that. My body doesn't do that. I mean, I get the heavy feeling, like I said, where it's hard to move, but I'm fully conscious and I'm not shaking or losing control of my muscles or anything. It's just the tiredness. And the voices and the monitoring thing, of course, but physically, my brain isn't short-circuiting like that. I've never passed out or had convulsions or anything even close to a seizure. So, no, definitely not. Just... just the other stuff.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a serious head injury?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't. I mean, I've bumped my head a few times over the years, like anyone else, right? I remember one time, gosh, I must have been maybe twelve or thirteen, and I was riding my bike down this huge hill near my childhood home—it was this really steep road, completely paved but just a massive drop—and I hit this patch of gravel near the bottom. I went flying right over the handlebars. Scraped up my knees something awful, like, completely ruined my favorite pair of jeans, and I definitely hit my head on the pavement. I was wearing a helmet, thank goodness, because my mom was always so strict about that. She wouldn't even let me out of the driveway without it. I remember the helmet actually cracked a little bit on the side, which just goes to show how hard I hit. \n\nBut even then, I didn't black out or anything. I was just really dazed and my ears were ringing for a few minutes. My mom took me to the urgent care just to be safe, and they shined the little light in my eyes and made me follow their finger, and they said I was totally fine. No concussion, no nothing. Just a headache that lasted for the rest of the evening. I just laid on the couch with an ice pack and watched TV. \n\nAnd other than that, I really can't think of anything. I've never been in a car accident where I hit the windshield or the steering wheel. I've never played any contact sports—I was always more of a band kid, honestly, played the trumpet for a few years before I realized I didn't actually like it. So I never played football or hockey where you're constantly getting tackled or checked into the boards. I've never fallen off a ladder or had anything heavy drop on me at work. \n\nSo, no, nothing serious at all. Just normal clumsy stuff, like standing up too fast and hitting my head on an open cabinet door in the kitchen. I did that just last week, actually, while I was trying to put away some coffee mugs. Hurt like crazy for a second, but definitely not a serious injury. My brain is physically fine, as far as I know. Never had any kind of trauma or anything that would cause damage up there. Just the regular bumps and bruises of life, you know?\n\nDoctor: Have there been times when other people said your behavior or speech seemed very disorganized or bizarre?\n\nPatient: Well, I... I don't think anyone has ever come right out and used the word \"bizarre,\" but I know I've had moments where things get a little weird. Like, when I get really overwhelmed—which happens more often lately with everything going on—I kind of just... short-circuit a little bit? I don't know how else to describe it. \n\nLike, the other day, I was making coffee, and I poured it, and I was standing there in the kitchen, and I just sort of froze with the mug halfway to my mouth. I don't even know how long I was standing there like that. My roommate, Greg—he works from home, so he's always around—he came into the kitchen to grab a snack, and he was like, \"Uh, dude, you good?\" And it totally snapped me out of it, but I realized my arm was actually starting to cramp because I'd been holding the mug in mid-air for so long. I just told him I was lost in thought, but it felt a lot heavier than that, you know? \n\nAnd then there are times when I catch myself just pacing. Not even pacing like I'm trying to figure something out, but just walking this same exact little loop in the living room, right by the edge of the couch and the coffee table. Over and over. I won't even realize I'm doing it until I notice the pattern on the rug looks blurry because I've been staring at the same spot on the floor for ten minutes straight. Greg has definitely noticed that, too. He'll be on his laptop on the couch, and he'll finally look up and say, \"Man, you're wearing a hole in the floor, what are you doing?\" And I never really have a good answer for him. I just say I'm restless, but it feels more like I'm just stuck on a loop and I can't figure out how to stop walking. \n\nSo, I don't know if that counts as disorganized or bizarre, but it's definitely not how I normally act. It's just... when the pressure builds up and the thoughts get too loud, my body just sort of does these weird, repetitive things without me really deciding to do them. It's kind of embarrassing, honestly, especially when someone else points it out, because then I realize how strange I must look. But it's not like I'm running around screaming or talking in gibberish or anything. It's more just... getting stuck. Like a record skipping.\n\nDoctor: For at least one month, have you been hearing the voice or feeling monitored on most days?\n\nPatient: Yeah, oh yeah, definitely for at least a month. It's been way longer than that, honestly. I've been trying to keep track, just in my head, you know, because it's hard to write things down when you feel like someone might find the notebook or something. But if I really sit down and trace it back, the voices and that whole feeling of being monitored, being tracked... it started, gosh, about a year and eight months ago. \n\nI remember because it was right around the time my lease was up at my old apartment, the one over on 4th Street, and I was dealing with all the stress of deciding whether to renew or find a new place with Greg. And at first, I thought maybe it was just the stress of moving, you know? Like, your brain plays tricks on you when you're packing boxes until 2 AM and living off takeout. But then we moved, and we got settled into the new place, and the feeling didn't go away. If anything, it got worse. The voices started getting more frequent, just this constant murmur in the background, commenting on what I was doing or telling me I was being watched. \n\nAnd the thing is, it's not just when I'm feeling really, really down. Like, I know I get those periods where everything is just heavy and dark and I can barely get out of bed—those depressive slumps, right? But the voices and the tracking stuff, they don't just happen then. They're there even when my mood is... well, relatively okay. Like, I could be having an alright day, maybe even managing to get some work done on the phones for customer service, talking to people about their billing issues, and I'll still hear it. I'll still feel that prickle on the back of my neck like the camera on my laptop is on, even when I've put a piece of tape over it. \n\nSo it's not just tied to the sadness. It's its own separate thing that just... runs parallel to it, I guess? Or sometimes they overlap and it's a total mess, but yeah, it's been a year and eight months of this, pretty much most days. It's exhausting, honestly, just carrying it around all the time, trying to pretend everything is normal when you're constantly looking over your shoulder or trying to tune out someone talking to you when there's nobody in the room. But yeah, definitely way more than a month.\n\nDoctor: Have people told you that your face or voice seems unusually flat or emotionless?\n\nPatient: Yeah, actually, they have. It's funny you ask that, because just a couple of weeks ago, my buddy Mark—we took some college classes together back in the day—he brought it up. He was telling me this whole long story about his dog needing surgery. And I mean, I love that dog, he's this super sweet golden retriever mix, so I really did care about what he was saying. But apparently, I wasn't reacting the way I was supposed to. Mark literally stopped in the middle of his story and was like, \"Dude, are you even listening? You're acting like a robot.\" He told me that my voice just sounds completely flat now, and that my face is totally hard to read. Like I'm just staring right through him. \n\nAnd the thing is, I was listening! I felt awful for the dog. But I guess the emotion just doesn't translate to the outside anymore. I just find myself sitting there, and even when I'm genuinely invested in the conversation, I mostly just give these really short answers. Just, like, \"Yeah,\" or \"Wow,\" or \"Okay.\" I just don't have the energy to put into the facial expressions or the tone of voice, you know? It's like the bridge between what I'm feeling inside and what my face is doing is just out of order. \n\nAnd honestly, because of that whole dynamic, it just makes hanging out with people feel like this massive, exhausting chore. So lately, I just avoid making plans altogether. Greg will ask if I want to go grab a drink down the street, or Mark will want to come over to watch a game, and I just make up some excuse to stay home. It's just so much easier to stay in my room than to have to constantly try and force myself to look animated so my friends don't think I'm ignoring them. I just... I don't have the battery for it anymore.\n\nDoctor: Since the voices and feeling monitored first began, has depression been present for most of that time?\n\nPatient: Well, if I really sit down and think about the whole timeline... you know, going back to that whole apartment move a year and eight months ago when the voices and the monitoring stuff really kicked off... yeah. Yeah, it pretty much has been there for the vast majority of it. It's almost like they moved in together, if that makes sense. \n\nBecause most of the time since this all started, there's just been this... this heavy, constant sadness just sitting there in the background of my life. And the guilt, too. I don't even know why I feel so guilty half the time, but I just carry this weight around like I've let everyone down or done something wrong, which just drains whatever energy I have left. So then I have zero interest in doing anything anyway. Like, I used to love playing video games with Greg or just doing normal stuff, and now I just... I don't care. At all. And my sleep—oh man, my sleep has been just completely messed up this whole time, just tossing and turning, never really getting any actual rest. \n\nBut it's like, even when I have a good week? Because there are weeks that are lighter, you know? Like, maybe work isn't quite as terrible with people yelling at me over their phone bills, and I think, 'Okay, maybe I'm finally snapping out of this slump.' But even during those lighter weeks, all of it—the sadness, the guilt, the sleep issues, the not caring about anything—it's still there, just kind of humming in the background, waiting for me to notice it again. So yeah, it's pretty much been a constant companion this whole entire time, just dragging along behind everything else."
